=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
For a non-empty string T consisting of A, B, and C, we call it a good string if it can be turned into an empty string by performing the following two types of operations any number of times in any order.

- Operation 1: Choose two identical characters in the string and delete them (cannot be performed if there are not two or more identical characters).
- Operation 2: Choose one A, one B, and one C in the string and delete them (cannot be performed if there are not one or more of each of A, B, and C).

For example, ABACA is a good string because it can be turned into an empty string by performing the operations as follows:

- Choose the 2nd, 4th, and 5th characters and delete them (Operation 2). The string becomes AA.
- Choose the 1st and 2nd characters and delete them (Operation 1). The string becomes an empty string.

You are given a string S of length N consisting of A, B, C, and ?. How many ways are there to replace each ? with A, B, or C to form a string that contains at least K good strings as contiguous substrings? Substrings are counted separately if they are at different positions in the original string, even if they are identical strings.
Find the count modulo 998244353.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
S

Output

Print the answer modulo 998244353.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 50
- 0 \leq K \leq \frac{N(N+1)}{2}
- N and K are integers.
- |S| = N
- S is a string consisting of A, B, C, and ?.

Sample Input 1

4 2
A?AB

Sample Output 1

1

By replacing ? with A, B, or C, we can obtain the following three strings: AAAB, ABAB, ACAB.
Among these, AAAB contains two good substrings: the AA at positions 1,2 and the AA at positions 2,3. Note that even if the substrings are identical as strings, they are counted separately if they are at different positions in the original string.
On the other hand, ABAB contains only one good substring ABAB. Also, ACAB contains only one good substring CAB.

Sample Input 2

50 411
??AB??C???????????????????????????????A???C????A??

Sample Output 2

457279314

Print the count modulo 998244353.

Sample Input 3

1 0
A

Sample Output 3

1

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now, DP details.

Let's define MOD = 998244353.

class_id = [min(i, i^7) for i in range(8)] with bits A=1,B=2,C=4. Let's compute:
0:0
1:1
2:2
3:3
4:3
5:2
6:1
7:0
Good.

Allowed transitions:
If ch == 'A': [1]
elif ch == 'B': [2]
elif ch == 'C': [4]
else: [1,2,4]

For each state s, ns = s ^ bit.

DP initialization:
dp = { (0, (1,0,0,0)): 1 }? Since p0 class0 count. But if we store state separately? Let's choose representation.

Option 1: dp = {(0, (1,0,0,0)): 1}. For each char:
  ndp = {}
  for (s, cnt), ways in dp.items():
    for bit in bits:
      ns = s ^ bit
      nc = list(cnt)
      nc[class_id[ns]] += 1
      key = (ns, tuple(nc))
      ndp[key] = (ndp.get(key,0)+ways) % MOD
  dp=ndp
At end:
ans=0
for (s,cnt), ways in dp.items():
  score = sum(c*(c-1)//2 for c in cnt)
  if score >= K: ans += ways
print(ans%MOD)

This is simple. But tuple key with state and counts. At each step, number of keys maybe up to 8*C. Need memory. We can reduce by using dp as list of dicts per state:
dp = [dict() for _ in range(8)]
dp[0][(1,0,0,0)] = 1
For char:
  ndp = [dict() for _ in range(8)]
  for s in range(8):
    for cnt, ways in dp[s].items():
      for bit in bits:
        ns=s^bit
        nc = list(cnt); nc[class_id[ns]] +=1; nc=tuple(nc)
        ndp[ns][nc] = (ndp[ns].get(nc,0)+ways)%MOD
  dp=ndp
This avoids state in key, maybe faster because iterate per state. But still creates list.

Could also store counts as tuple of length 4. Updating by list conversion each transition. Number transitions 30M; list conversion 30M maybe okay but maybe heavy. We can optimize by precomputing for each class count tuple? Hmm.

Alternative represent counts as a single integer base (N+2) or encode 4 counts into integer to avoid tuple overhead. Since counts sum <=51, each count <=51. We can encode as c0 + c1*B + c2*B^2 + c3*B^3 with B=52 (or 64). Then update count by adding increment = B^class. Decode at end. This could be faster and memory less. Let's consider.

Let BASE = N+2 maybe 52, but counts can be 51, so base 52 works. Encode counts as c0 + c1*BASE + c2*BASE^2 + c3*BASE^3. Add one to class j: code += pow_base[j] where pow_base[j]=BASE^j. Since no carry because count < BASE. At end decode counts from code. This avoids tuple allocation. DP key = (state, code) or per state dict code->ways. At end decode code to counts. This is efficient.

Then DP:
dp = [dict() for _ in range(8)]
dp[0][1] = 1  # code for (1,0,0,0)
For each ch:
  ndp = [dict() for _ in range(8)]
  bits = ...
  for s in range(8):
    d = dp[s]
    if not d: continue
    for code, ways in d.items():
      for bit in bits:
        ns = s ^ bit
        nc_code = code + powB[class_id[ns]]
        nd = ndp[ns]
        nd[nc_code] = (nd.get(nc_code, 0) + ways) % MOD
  dp = ndp
At end:
ans=0
for s in range(8):
  for code, ways in dp[s].items():
    c0 = code % BASE
    c1 = (code // BASE) % BASE
    c2 = (code // (BASE*BASE)) % BASE
    c3 = code // (BASE*BASE*BASE)
    score = c0*(c0-1)//2 + c1*(c1-1)//2 + c2*(c2-1)//2 + c3*(c3-1)//2
    if score >= K: ans = (ans + ways) % MOD
print(ans%MOD)

This is likely faster.

Let's verify characterization with prefix classes more formally:
For a string T, let pref_i counts mod2. Substring T[l..r] counts parity = pref_r xor pref_{l-1}. Good iff this vector is 000 or 111. That is equivalent to pref_r = pref_{l-1} or pref_r = pref_{l-1} xor 111. The equivalence classes under "same or complement" are the four pairs. Thus good substrings count = sum over classes C(cnt,2). Good.

Now, implement:

import sys
MOD=998244353

def main():
    data=sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data: return
    N=int(data[0]); K=int(data[1]); S=data[2].strip()
    q=S.count('?')
    if K==0:
        print(pow(3,q,MOD))
        return
    BASE=N+2
    powB=[1]
    for _ in range(3):
        powB.append(powB[-1]*BASE)
    cls=[0]*8
    for st in range(8):
        cls[st]=min(st, st^7)
    dp=[{} for _ in range(8)]
    dp[0][1]=1  # count class0 = 1
    for ch in S:
        if ch=='A':
            bits=(1,)
        elif ch=='B':
            bits=(2,)
        elif ch=='C':
            bits=(4,)
        else:
            bits=(1,2,4)
        ndp=[{} for _ in range(8)]
        for s in range(8):
            d=dp[s]
            if not d:
                continue
            for code, ways in d.items():
                for bit in bits:
                    ns=s ^ bit
                    add=powB[cls[ns]]
                    ncode=code+add
                    nd=ndp[ns]
                    val=nd.get(ncode,0)+ways
                    if val>=MOD:
                        val-=MOD
                    nd[ncode]=val
        dp=ndp
    ans=0
    B1=BASE
    B2=BASE*BASE
    B3=BASE*BASE*BASE
    for s in range(8):
        for code, ways in dp[s].items():
            c0=code % BASE
            c1=(code // BASE) % BASE
            c2=(code // B2) % BASE
            c3=code // B3
            score=c0*(c0-1)//2 + c1*(c1-1)//2 + c2*(c2-1)//2 + c3*(c3-1)//2
            if score >= K:
                ans += ways
                if ans >= MOD:
                    ans -= MOD  # But ans+ways <2MOD? ans maintained <MOD, ways<MOD, yes one subtraction enough. If multiple loops, after each addition subtract once. Good.
    print(ans % MOD)

if __name__=="__main__": main()

Now, let's think about proof of class relation:
Let v_i = prefix parity vector. For substring (l,r), parity vector d = v_r - v_{l-1} mod2 = v_r xor v_{l-1}. Good iff d ∈ {000,111}. This iff v_r ∈ {v_{l-1}, v_{l-1} xor 111}. Define relation R: u~v iff u=v or u=v xor111. This is equivalence because xor111 is involution and partitions into 4 pairs. Thus count good substrings = number of pairs of prefix positions whose states are in same pair. Good.

Let's consider if there is a more subtle issue: The good string condition depends only on counts parity, but the number of good substrings in a string is sum over classes C(cnt,2). However, is every pair of prefix positions in the same class corresponding to a good substring? Let's prove:
Let prefix parity vectors u and v. Substring parity = v xor u. If u and v are in same class, then either v=u -> parity 000, or v=u xor111 -> parity 111. Both are all equal. Conversely, if substring good, parity d is 000 or111, so v = u xor d, so v=u or u xor111. Thus same class. Good.

Let's implement with precomputed transitions:
bits_all = (1,2,4)
trans = [[s ^ b for b in bits_all] for s in range(8)]
add = [[powB[cls[ns]] for ns in trans[s]] for s in range(8)]
Then for char:

Then inner:
for idx in allowed_indices:
  ns = trans[s][idx]
  ncode = code + add[s][idx]
This is good.

But note trans[s] for bits_all order A,B,C. If ch fixed, allowed_indices accordingly. Good.

Let's code:

trans = []
add = []
for s in range(8):
    t = [s ^ 1, s ^ 2, s ^ 4]
    trans.append(t)
    add.append([powB[cls[ns]] for ns in t])

allowed = {'A': (0,), 'B': (1,), 'C': (2,), '?': (0,1,2)}

Then loop:
for ch in S:
    idxs = allowed[ch]
    ndp = [{} for _ in range(8)]
    for s in range(8):
        d = dp[s]
        if not d: continue
        ts = trans[s]
        ads = add[s]
        for code, ways in d.items():
            for idx in idxs:
                ns = ts[idx]
                ncode = code + ads[idx]
                nd = ndp[ns]
                val = nd.get(ncode, 0) + ways
                if val >= MOD:
                    val -= MOD
                nd[ncode] = val
    dp = ndp

This is efficient.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "trans" and "add" if bits order. Good.

Let's write final code carefully:

import sys

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    N = int(data[0])
    K = int(data[1])
    S = data[2]

    q = S.count('?')
    if K == 0:
        print(pow(3, q, MOD))
        return

    BASE = N + 2
    powB = [1]
    for _ in range(3):
        powB.append(powB[-1] * BASE)

    cls = [0] * 8
    for st in range(8):
        cls[st] = min(st, st ^ 7)

    trans = []
    add = []
    for st in range(8):
        t = [st ^ 1, st ^ 2, st ^ 4]
        trans.append(t)
        add.append([powB[cls[ns]] for ns in t])

    dp = [dict() for _ in range(8)]
    dp[0][1] = 1

    allowed = {'A': (0,), 'B': (1,), 'C': (2,), '?': (0, 1, 2)}

    for ch in S:
        idxs = allowed[ch]
        ndp = [dict() for _ in range(8)]
        for st in range(8):
            d = dp[st]
            if not d:
                continue
            t = trans[st]
            a = add[st]
            for code, ways in d.items():
                for idx in idxs:
                    ns = t[idx]
                    ncode = code + a[idx]
                    nd = ndp[ns]
                    val = nd.get(ncode, 0) + ways
                    if val >= MOD:
                        val -= MOD
                    nd[ncode] = val
        dp = ndp

    B1 = BASE
    B2 = BASE * BASE
    B3 = BASE * BASE * BASE
    ans = 0
    for st in range(8):
        for code, ways in dp[st].items():
            c0 = code % B1
            c1 = (code // B1) % B1
            c2 = (code // B2) % B1
            c3 = code // B3
            score = c0 * (c0 - 1) // 2
            score += c1 * (c1 - 1) // 2
            score += c2 * (c2 - 1) // 2
            score += c3 * (c3 - 1) // 2
            if score >= K:
                ans += ways
                if ans >= MOD:
                    ans -= MOD
    print(ans % MOD)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Need check if `c3 = code // B3` should be `% B1`? Since c3 < B1, no need. But for safety, `c3 = (code // B3) % B1`. Could include. It won't hurt. If code//B3 could be >B1 due to carry? No, but include for clarity. Let's include `% B1` maybe. But if code//B3 = c3 + carry? no carry. If include, same. Good.

Potential issue: In final ans accumulation, if ans + ways >= 2*MOD? ans<MOD, ways<MOD, sum<2MOD, one subtraction. Good. But if we do `ans % MOD` at end, even if not subtract, okay. But we subtract. Good.
